<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that the chromatic quasisymmetric function of every natural unit interval graph is elementary-positive over $`\mathbb N[q]`$. This resolves the elementary-positivity part of the Shareshian–Wachs conjecture.

- **claim-main-theorem** (theorem, [source](#stmt-main-theorem)): There is a deterministic terminating procedure which, given a natural unit interval graph $G$ on $[n]$ and $\sigma\in D_G^0$, returns a partition $\theta_G(\sigma)\vdash n$ such that $$\chi_G(X;q)=\sum_{\sigma\in D_G^0} q^{\mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G(\sigma)}e_{\theta_G(\sigma)}(X). \label{main:witness}$$ In particular every elementary coefficient of $\chi_G(X;q)$ belongs to $\mathbb N[q]$. The assertion holds as a formal symmetric-function identity, uniformly in $n$ and $G$.
  - Explanation: A deterministic terminating procedure assigns to each graph-nondescent permutation a partition, so that the chromatic quasisymmetric function is a sum over permutations of a graph-inversion weight times an elementary function. Hence every elementary coefficient has nonnegative integer coefficients in q.
- **claim-hessenberg-modules** (theorem, [source](#stmt-main-hessenberg)): Let $n\ge1$, let $h$ be as above with graph $G=G(h)$, and let $S\in M_n(\mathbb C)$ be regular semisimple. Write $$\operatorname{Hess}(S,h)= \{F_\bullet\in\operatorname{Flag}(\mathbb C^n): SF_i\subseteq F_{h(i)}\text{ for all }i\}.$$ For $\lambda=(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_\ell)\vdash n$, let $M^\lambda=\operatorname{Ind}_{S_\lambda}^{S_n}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb C}$ be the Young permutation module, where $S_\lambda=S_{\lambda_1}\times\cdots\times S_{\lambda_\ell}$ is the standard Young subgroup, and put $$m_{\lambda,d}(h)= \#\{\sigma\in D_G^0:\theta_G(\sigma)=\lambda,\ \mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G(\sigma)=d\}.$$ Then for every integer $d\ge0$, under Tymoczko’s dot action on ordinary cohomology there is an abstract $\mathbb C[S_n]$-module isomorphism $$H^{2d}(\operatorname{Hess}(S,h);\mathbb C) \cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash n}(M^\lambda)^{\oplus m_{\lambda,d}(h)}.$$
  - Explanation: Each graded piece of the cohomology of a regular semisimple Hessenberg variety is, as an abstract module of the symmetric group under the dot action, a direct sum of Young permutation modules whose multiplicities count witness permutations.
- **claim-insertion-digits** (theorem, [source](#stmt-graph-digits)): There is a bijection between $\mathcal A_G$ and the sequences $(j_1,\ldots,j_n)$ with $0\le j_a\le l_a$. Under this bijection, $\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}_G(w)=\sum_a j_a$. Reversal is a bijection $D_G^0\to\mathcal A_G$, and $$\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}_G(\operatorname{reverse}\sigma)=M-\mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G(\sigma). \label{graph:reverse}$$
  - Explanation: Admissible words are in bijection with bounded insertion-digit sequences, the digit sum gives the edge-inversion count, and word reversal exchanges the two permutation domains while complementing inversions.
- **claim-commuting-sums** (theorem, [source](#stmt-graph-commutation)): For every natural unit interval graph $\Gamma$ and all $j,k\ge0$, $E_j(\Gamma)E_k(\Gamma)=E_k(\Gamma)E_j(\Gamma)$.
  - Explanation: The independent-set sums of a natural unit interval graph commute in the quantum torus, shown by a weight-preserving exchange on path components.
- **claim-coloring-kernel** (theorem, [source](#stmt-graph-kernel)): If $\alpha(\Gamma)\le D$, then $X_G(X;q)$ is symmetric and $$X_G(X;v^2)=\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash n\\\ell(\lambda)\le D}} v^M[X_u]m_\lambda(Y)\,e_\lambda(X). \label{graph:coefficient}$$ The equality is independent of the extra vertices of $\Gamma$.
  - Explanation: When independent sets are bounded in size, the coloring generating function is symmetric and its elementary coefficients are centered coefficients of evaluated monomial symmetric functions.
- **claim-color-reversal** (theorem, [source](#stmt-graph-reciprocity)): For every natural unit interval graph $G$, $X_G(X;q)=\chi_G(X;q)$. Writing $c_\lambda(q)=[e_\lambda]X_G(X;q)$, one has $$c_\lambda(q)=q^M c_\lambda(q^{-1}) \label{graph:reciprocity-eq}$$ for every partition $\lambda$.
  - Explanation: Reversing colors identifies the inversion generating function with the chromatic quasisymmetric function and gives a reciprocity law for every elementary coefficient.
- **claim-positive-reordering** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-reordering)): Suppose an admissible product of elementary units is written in ray order, with $\Omega(p_i,p_j)>0$ whenever the ray of $p_i$ precedes the distinct ray of $p_j$. Its factorization in the opposite ray order is again a product of elementary units, with nonnegative integral multiplicities. The output list is admissible.* *Every coefficient at fixed exponent and fixed $v$-degree is a finite sum of nonnegative integers, and each exponent has bounded upper $v$-degree. The assertion also holds modulo any fixed charge cutoff. For the algorithmic assertion, assume that lattice operations, the form $\Omega$ and the charge are effectively computable, that input occurrences at bounded charge and above a given parameter threshold can be listed effectively, and that for every $B$ one can compute an integer $A_B\ge1$ such that $$\|\Omega(p,q)\|\le A_B \qquad\text{whenever }p,q\in C,\quad c(p),c(q)\le B.$$ Then every prescribed coefficient and every bounded part of the output unit list can be calculated by finite rational linear algebra.
  - Explanation: An admissible product of elementary units in one ray order refactors in the opposite ray order into elementary units with nonnegative integral multiplicities and an admissible list; coefficients are finite sums and are effectively computable under stated bounds.
- **claim-ordered-decomposition** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-ordered)): Choose graded vector-space sections $B_d\to S_d$ of all quotient maps. For every $d$, multiplication induces a vector-space isomorphism $$\label{alg:ordered-sum} S_d\cong \bigoplus_{\substack{d^{(1)}+\cdots+d^{(m)}=d\\ \mu(d^{(1)})>\cdots>\mu(d^{(m)})}} B_{d^{(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes B_{d^{(m)}},$$ with the polynomial-degree shifts prescribed by the shuffle kernels.
  - Explanation: Shuffle spaces decompose as a direct sum, over lists of strictly decreasing slope, of tensor products of the semistable quotient spaces.
- **claim-slope-restrictions** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-restrictions)): If all $r_\ell$ have the slope of $d$, Equation <a href="#alg:restriction-S" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:restriction-S">[alg:restriction-S]</a> factors through a map $$\label{alg:restriction} R_P^z:B_d\longrightarrow \left(\bigotimes_\ell B_{r_\ell}\right)[z].$$ These maps compose under refinement, with the successive centers added. When a same-slope product is restricted, only splits whose nonempty subgroups have this slope survive; the restrictions of its two factors may already be taken in their respective quotients.
  - Explanation: On a single slope the separated-center restriction maps descend to the quotient spaces, compose under refinement, and only same-slope splits survive on products.
- **claim-symbol-detection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-detection)): Every $L_P(f)$ is polynomial in its centers. The maps $L_P$ jointly inject $\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}_wB_d$ into their polynomial targets. For the additive tensor filtration $$\label{alg:tensor-filtration} \mathcal F_w(B_a\otimes B_b) =\sum_{u+v\ge w}\mathcal F_uB_a\otimes\mathcal F_vB_b,$$ tensor products of the restriction tests characterize membership, and tensor products $L_R\otimes L_S$ jointly detect its associated grades.
  - Explanation: The restriction symbols are polynomial in their centers and jointly detect the associated graded pieces, including on tensor products with the additive filtration.
- **claim-product-symbol** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-product-symbol)): The same-slope product preserves the sum of filtration weights. For $f\in\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}_uB_d$, $g\in\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}_vB_e$, and $R=(r_1,\ldots,r_m)$, its leading symbols satisfy $$\label{alg:product-symbol-formula} L_R(f*g)= \sum_{\substack{r_i=d_i'+e_i'\\ \sum_i d_i'=d,\ \sum_i e_i'=e}} (-1)^{\sum_{i>j}\chi(d_i',e_j')} (*^{\otimes R}) \bigl(L_{(d_i')}(f)\otimes L_{(e_i')}(g)\bigr).$$ All nonzero entries in this sum have slope $\theta$; zero entries in a list of symbols are omitted. The operator $*^{\otimes R}$ places the two factors belonging to each target group next to each other, without a sign, and applies its shuffle product.
  - Explanation: The same-slope product preserves total filtration weight, and its leading symbol is a signed sum over splittings of tensor-then-shuffle terms.
- **claim-graded-coproduct** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-coproduct)): For $d=a+b$ on the fixed slope there is a map $$\Delta_{a,b}:\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}B_d\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}B_a\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}B_b$$ preserving both original shifted degree and total filtration weight, characterized by $$\label{alg:coproduct-symbol} (L_R\otimes L_S)(\Delta_{a,b}f)=L_{R,S}(f)$$ for all lists $R,S$ of $a,b$, with independent centers and with the lists concatenated on the right. When a dimension is zero its component is the usual identity tensor unit.
  - Explanation: A coproduct component on the associated graded space is built by evaluating a Laurent expansion at zero separation; it is characterized by its symbols on concatenated center lists.
- **claim-bialgebra** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-bialgebra)): With $\Delta=\sum_{a+b=d}\Delta_{a,b}$, $\mathcal H$ is a connected dimension-graded bialgebra, and its coproduct is cocommutative with the interchange sign $\varepsilon(a,b)$.
  - Explanation: With the summed coproduct the associated graded space is a connected dimension-graded bialgebra whose coproduct is cocommutative up to the interchange sign.
- **claim-signed-pbw** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-pbw)): Symmetrized multiplication gives an isomorphism of graded vector spaces $$\label{alg:pbw-map} \operatorname{Sym}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal P) \longrightarrow\mathcal H.$$ Here the left side is the signed symmetric vector space: exchanging homogeneous vectors of dimensions $a,b$ multiplies a tensor by $\varepsilon(a,b)$. The gradings retained are dimension, original shifted degree, and filtration weight; tensor length is summed over.
  - Explanation: Symmetrized multiplication identifies the signed symmetric space on the primitive subspace with the whole bialgebra as graded vector spaces, via a convolution logarithm.
- **claim-primitive-strings** (theorem, [source](#stmt-alg-strings)): For each nonzero dimension $d$, the primitive space is a direct sum of strings with original shifted degrees $$h,h+1,h+2,\ldots, \qquad h-s(d)\in\mathbb N.$$ Each degree has finite dimension. Every vector in these strings has self-interchange sign $(-1)^{\chi(d,d)}$.
  - Explanation: In each nonzero dimension the primitive space is a direct sum of strings of consecutive shifted degrees with finite-dimensional pieces and a uniform self-interchange sign.
- **claim-wall-unit** (theorem, [source](#stmt-wall-unit)): For $j\ge0$, $$D_p= \frac{v^{-j^2}}{\prod_{i=1}^j(1-v^{-2i})}. \label{wall:unit-coefficient}$$ Let $t=\Omega(a,m)$. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(U_{a,k,f})X_m =X_m\frac{U_{a,k,f}(v^{2t}X_a)}{U_{a,k,f}(X_a)}. \label{wall:unit-ratio}$$ For $t>0$ the ratio is $\prod_{\ell=1}^t f(v^{k+2\ell}X_a)$. For $t<0$ the inverse adjoint has ratio $\prod_{\ell=t+1}^{0}f(v^{k+2\ell}X_a)$. Thus $\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(U_{a,k,f})^\varepsilon X_m$ has nonnegative integral Laurent-series coefficients whenever $\varepsilon t\ge0$. Its constant, or no-jump, coefficient is one.
  - Explanation: The simple unit has an explicit coefficient formula, and conjugating a monomial by an elementary unit gives a finite product with nonnegative integral coefficients in the positive direction and constant term one.
- **claim-wall-factors** (theorem, [source](#stmt-wall-factors)): At every generic point of a wall, and to every specified root order, its element is a product of elementary units on its ray with the finiteness conditions above. The same conclusion holds if the simple incoming data are replaced by allowed products of elementary units. If a crossing has the sign of $-V(m)$ on its wall normal, the resulting monomial jump from $X_m$ has coefficients in $\mathbb N((v^{-1}))$.
  - Explanation: Every wall element, to any specified root order, factors into elementary units along its ray, and monomial jumps in the positive crossing direction have coefficients with nonnegative integer entries.
- **claim-coefficient-recursion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-wall-recursion)): Fix a generic target chamber $H$, represented by $h$, and a target exponent $n$. At sufficient finite root order, follow $h+sV(n)$ from sufficiently large $s$ down to $s=0$. Let its successive crossings be $W_i:I_i\longrightarrow I_{i+1}$, with primitive positive wall normal $p_i$. Define $$J_i(a,j)=[X_{a+jp_i}]W_i(X_a),\qquad j\ge0.$$ Then $J_i(a,0)=1$, and for the exponents $a=n-jp_i$ that can contribute one has $J_i(a,j)\in\mathbb N((v^{-1}))$. Moreover, $$F(H)=A_n(F)+ \sum_i\ \sum_{\substack{j>0\\n-jp_i\ \text{in the support bound}}} J_i(n-jp_i,j)[X_{n-jp_i}]F(I_i). \label{wall:coefficient-recursion}$$ All sums are finite at a fixed exponent. With the simple data <a href="#wall:initial-unit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wall:initial-unit">[wall:initial-unit]</a>, the jump coefficients are effectively computable at every fixed energy by finite rational arithmetic and Laurent expansion in $v^{-1}$. The same holds for the section coefficients when its initial value is a specified finite Laurent polynomial, or when its incoming data are finitely specified rational functions of $v$.
  - Explanation: Following a line from the incoming direction to a target chamber expresses the chamber value as the incoming coefficient plus positive jump coefficients times earlier coefficients; this is effectively computable at fixed energy.
- **claim-positive-sections** (theorem, [source](#stmt-wall-positivity)): Every $\Theta_m$ has coefficients in $\mathbb N((v^{-1}))$ in every chamber. More generally, the same is true of a section $F$ all of whose incoming coefficients belong to $\mathbb N((v^{-1}))$.
  - Explanation: Every theta section, and more generally every section with positive incoming coefficients, has nonnegative integer coefficients in every chamber.
- **claim-positive-products** (theorem, [source](#stmt-wall-product)): The structure coefficients in $$\Theta_{m_1}\cdots\Theta_{m_b} =\sum_n A_n(\Theta_{m_1}\cdots\Theta_{m_b})\Theta_n$$ belong to $\mathbb N((v^{-1}))$. Products and expansions are locally finite at every specified exponent.
  - Explanation: Products of theta sections expand in theta sections with positive structure coefficients, locally finitely at each exponent.
- **claim-fiber-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-wall-fiber-bound)): On the open side $\operatorname{sign}h(p)=\sigma$, a non-pure root $kp+u$ occurring in a wall element or its logarithm satisfies $$0\le k\le M_\sigma(u). \label{wall:fiber-bound-equation}$$ The pure $p$-wall has element $D_p$ everywhere on the cut where no other wall direction is present.
  - Explanation: On either open side of the cut, the multiplicity of the mutating root in any non-pure root of a wall element is bounded by an explicit function of the remaining coordinates; the pure wall carries the simple unit.
- **claim-change-of-chart** (theorem, [source](#stmt-wall-mutation)): The wall system for $C$, with simple data $D_c$, is carried to the wall system for $C'$, with simple data $D_{c'}$, as follows. Off the cut $p^\perp$, use the covector map $T$ from <a href="#wall:piecewise-mutation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wall:piecewise-mutation">[wall:piecewise-mutation]</a> and act on exponents by $L_\sigma$ on the side of sign $\sigma$. On the cut, replace the pure wall by $D_{-p}$.* *Suppose $F$ and $F'$ are sections specified by finite Laurent polynomials in the respective all-negative chambers, and these initial polynomials satisfy the rational identity $$F=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(D_p)F'. \label{wall:initial-comparison}$$ For $h(p)>0$ their transported values agree coefficientwise under the identity; for $h(p)<0$ they agree coefficientwise under $S$. In particular, $$A_n(F)=A_{\mu_p(n)}(F'),\qquad \mu_p(n)=n+[-\Omega(p,n)]_+p. \label{wall:incoming-mutation}$$ The map $\mu_p:M\to M$ is a bijection.
  - Explanation: Mutation carries the wall system of one chart to that of the mutated chart, matches actual finite coefficients of polynomial sections, and induces a bijection on incoming labels.
- **claim-embedding** (construction, [source](#stmt-tri-embedding)): Every naturally labeled unit interval graph on $n$ vertices occurs as an ordered induced subgraph on $n$ selected bridges of a triangular chart. The times and phases may all be chosen rationally.
  - Explanation: Every naturally labeled unit interval graph appears as an ordered induced subgraph on selected bridges of a triangular chart, with rational times and phases.
- **claim-rotation-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tri-rotation-comparison)): Let $Y'$ be the alphabet after one rotation. For every symmetric polynomial $P$, $$\label{tri:polynomial-comparison} P(Y)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(D_p)P(Y').$$ Both sides are finite Laurent polynomials, so the actual incoming coefficients satisfy $$\label{tri:incoming-rotation} A_m(P(Y))=A_{m+[-\Omega(p,m)]_+p}(P(Y')).$$
  - Explanation: One rotation conjugates every evaluated symmetric polynomial by the simple unit and shifts incoming coefficients by the corresponding piecewise-linear map.
- **claim-stationary-labels** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tri-stationary)): Let $P$ be a homogeneous symmetric polynomial of degree $N$, and suppose $A_m(P(Y))\ne0$. Write the label in the original vertex basis as $$m=\sum_{i=1}^D\nu_i d_i+\sum_{j\leq i}\kappa_{j,i}b_{j,i}, \qquad K_i=\sum_{j=1}^i\kappa_{j,i},\qquad K_0=K_D=0.$$ Then $$\label{tri:stationary-conditions} K_i=0\quad(1\leq i<D),\qquad \xi_{j,i}:=\Omega(b_{j,i}-d_i,m)\geq0\quad(1\leq j\leq i<D).$$ The lattice label $m$ is fixed by every tropical step in Equation <a href="#tri:incoming-rotation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tri:incoming-rotation">[tri:incoming-rotation]</a>. If $m$ is pure, meaning that all its bridge coordinates vanish, then $m=\sum_i\mu_i d_i$, where $\mu_1\geq\cdots\geq\mu_D\geq0$ and $\sum_i\mu_i=N$.
  - Explanation: A nonvanishing incoming label of an evaluated homogeneous symmetric polynomial has vanishing level sums of bridge coordinates and nonnegative pairings, is fixed by all rotation steps, and if pure is a dominant anchor combination.
- **claim-pure-extraction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tri-pure)): Let $P$ be a homogeneous symmetric polynomial of degree $N$, and let $m=\sum_i\mu_i d_i$ with $\mu_1\geq\cdots\geq\mu_D\geq0$ and $\sum_i\mu_i=N$. For commuting indeterminates $z_1,\ldots,z_D$, $$\label{tri:pure-formula} A_m(P(Y))=[z_1^{\mu_1}\cdots z_D^{\mu_D}]P(z_1,\ldots,z_D).$$
  - Explanation: At a pure dominant label the incoming coefficient equals the ordinary monomial coefficient of the corresponding commuting-variable polynomial.
- **claim-linear-section** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tri-linear)): In the all-negative chamber of the triangular chart, $$U=\Theta_{d_1}.$$
  - Explanation: The degree-one evaluated sum equals the theta section at the first anchor in the all-negative chamber, proved by a gap recurrence.
- **claim-vertex-domination** (bound, [source](#stmt-tri-domination)): For every partition $\mu\vdash N$ with at most $D$ parts, padded by zeroes, put $x_\mu=\sum_i\mu_i d_i$. In the all-negative chamber, $\Theta_{x_\mu}$ has nonnegative vertex exponents and coefficients in $\mathbb N[v,v^{-1}]$, and is a finite Laurent polynomial. More precisely, coefficientwise in both vertex exponents and $v$, $$\label{tri:positive-domination} \frac{N!}{\prod_i\mu_i!}\,\Theta_{x_\mu}\ \leq\ U^N.$$
  - Explanation: Pure theta sections are finite Laurent polynomials with nonnegative vertex exponents and are coefficientwise dominated, up to a multinomial factor, by a power of the linear section.
- **claim-monomial-sections** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tri-monomial)): For every partition $\lambda$ with at most $D$ parts, padded by zeroes to length $D$, one has in the all-negative chamber $$\label{tri:monomial-identity} m_\lambda(Y)=\Theta_{\sum_{i=1}^D\lambda_i d_i}.$$ In particular, $$\label{tri:elementary-identity} E_k=\Theta_{d_1+\cdots+d_k}\qquad(0\leq k\leq D).$$
  - Explanation: Each evaluated monomial symmetric function equals a single theta section on anchors in the all-negative chamber, and in particular each evaluated elementary function does.
- **claim-elementary-positivity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tri-positivity)): Let $G$ be a natural unit interval graph on $n$ vertices, and put $M=\|E(G)\|$. In its elementary expansion $$\chi_G(X;q)=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}c_\lambda(q)e_\lambda(X),$$ every $c_\lambda(q)$ belongs to $\mathbb N[q]$ and has degree at most $M$. For a triangular embedding with $D\geq n$, let $u$ be the sum of the selected vertex exponents and set $x_\lambda=\sum_i\lambda_i d_i$, padding $\lambda$ by zeroes. Then $$\label{tri:positive-coefficient} [X_u]\Theta_{x_\lambda}^{H_-} =v^{-M}c_\lambda(v^2) \in\sum_{d=0}^{M}\mathbb Nv^{2d-M},$$ where $H_-$ is the all-negative chamber.
  - Explanation: Every elementary coefficient of the chromatic quasisymmetric function lies in the nonnegative integer polynomials with degree at most the edge count, and it is read off a theta section coefficient.
- **claim-effective-slices** (bound, [source](#stmt-route-finite)): At fixed total exponent in $B$ and fixed total energy, every packet decomposition and local jump decomposition used below is a finite set that can be enumerated effectively. The same holds for a sequence of at most $h(R)$ nonzero total jumps.
  - Explanation: At fixed exponent and energy all packet and jump decompositions form finite sets that can be enumerated effectively, including bounded sequences of nonzero jumps.
- **claim-packet-switch** (construction, [source](#stmt-route-switch)): There is a computable energy-preserving bijection $$\label{route:switch-equation} \left\{\text{product states of exponent $y$ in $II$}\right\} \longleftrightarrow \left\{\begin{array}{c} \text{product states of exponent $z$ in $I$, together with}\\ \text{a decorated jump of $W$ from $z$ to $y$} \end{array}\right\}.$$ On the right the energy is the state energy plus the jump energy. If $\Omega(p,y)=0$, the total jump is always the unique zero jump.
  - Explanation: Across one wall crossing there is a computable energy-preserving bijection between product states on one side and product states on the other side together with a decorated jump.
- **claim-return-to-anchors** (construction, [source](#stmt-route-return)): A nonempty terminal product state has label $x=x_\mu=\sum_{i=1}^D\mu_i d_i$ for a partition $\mu\vdash N$, padded by zeroes. Keeping its total history fixed, it can be transported bijectively to an ordered tuple of anchor subsets of sizes $k_1,\ldots,k_b$ and total exponent $x_\mu$. Every such anchor tuple has energy zero.
  - Explanation: A nonempty terminal state has a dominant anchor label and, keeping its history, is transported bijectively to an ordered tuple of anchor subsets of zero energy.
- **claim-degree-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-route-parity)): For every partition $\mu\vdash N$, histories in $T_{x_\mu}$ have $$\label{route:parity-range} -M\leq E(t)\leq M,\qquad E(t)\equiv M\pmod 2.$$ Consequently $T_{x_\mu}$ is finite, and $v^M[X_u]\Theta_{x_\mu}^{H_-}$ belongs to $\mathbb N[q]$ with $q=v^2$ and degree at most $M$.
  - Explanation: History energies lie between minus and plus the edge count with the parity of the edge count, so the history sets are finite and the normalized coefficient is a polynomial with nonnegative integer coefficients.
- **claim-exact-mask** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-route-exact)): For every $J\subseteq[N-1]$ there is a computable energy-preserving bijection $\Phi_J$ between the two sides of Equation <a href="#route:required-bijection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="route:required-bijection">[route:required-bijection]</a> with their masks required to be *exactly* $J$. Both directions terminate on every input.
  - Explanation: Required-mask bijections are upgraded to exact-mask bijections by following alternating paths in two matchings, which terminates in both directions and preserves energy.
- **claim-permutation-witness** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-route-witness)): For every naturally labeled unit interval graph $G$, the preceding finite rule assigns a partition $\Lambda_G(w)\vdash n$ to each $w\in\mathcal A_G$ and satisfies the formal polynomial identity $$\label{route:witness-identity} X_G(X;q)=\sum_{w\in\mathcal A_G} q^{\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}_G(w)}e_{\Lambda_G(w)}(X).$$ In particular, every elementary coefficient is in $\mathbb N[q]$ and has degree at most $\|E(G)\|$.
  - Explanation: The finite rule assigns a partition to each admissible word and yields the formal identity expanding the inversion generating function in elementary functions with nonnegative integer coefficients of bounded degree.

        "anchor": "stmt-alg-reordering",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose an admissible product of elementary units is written in ray order, with $\\Omega(p_i,p_j)>0$ whenever the ray of $p_i$ precedes the distinct ray of $p_j$.         "exact_quote": "For $j\\ge0$, $$D_p=\n \\frac{v^{-j^2}}{\\prod_{i=1}^j(1-v^{-2i})}.\n \\label{wall:unit-coefficient}$$ Let $t=\\Omega(a,m)$. For $t<0$ the inverse adjoint has ratio $\\prod_{\\ell=t+1}^{0}f(v^{k+2\\ell}X_a)$.         "anchor": "stmt-wall-recursion",
        "exact_quote": "Fix a generic target chamber $H$, represented by $h$, and a target exponent $n$. At sufficient finite root order, follow $h+sV(n)$ from sufficiently large $s$ down to $s=0$. Let its successive crossings be $W_i:I_i\\longrightarrow I_{i+1}$, with primitive positive wall normal $p_i$. Define $$J_i(a,j)=[X_{a+jp_i}]W_i(X_a),\\qquad j\\ge0.$$ Then $J_i(a,0)=1$, and for the exponents $a=n-jp_i$ that can contribute one has $J_i(a,j)\\in\\mathbb N((v^{-1}))$. Moreover, $$F(H)=A_n(F)+\n \\sum_i\\ \\sum_{\\substack{j>0\\\\n-jp_i\\ \\text{in the support bound}}}\n J_i(n-jp_i,j)[X_{n-jp_i}]F(I_i).\n \\label{wall:coefficient-recursion}$$ All sums are finite at a fixed exponent. With the simple data <a href=\"#wall:initial-unit\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"wall:initial-unit\">[wall:initial-unit]</a>, the jump coefficients are effectively computable at every fixed energy by finite rational arithmetic and Laurent expansion in $v^{-1}$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-tri-stationary",
        "exact_quote": "Let $P$ be a homogeneous symmetric polynomial of degree $N$, and suppose $A_m(P(Y))\\ne0$. Write the label in the original vertex basis as $$m=\\sum_{i=1}^D\\nu_i d_i+\\sum_{j\\leq i}\\kappa_{j,i}b_{j,i},\n \\qquad K_i=\\sum_{j=1}^i\\kappa_{j,i},\\qquad K_0=K_D=0.$$ Then $$\\label{tri:stationary-conditions}\n K_i=0\\quad(1\\leq i<D),\\qquad\n \\xi_{j,i}:=\\Omega(b_{j,i}-d_i,m)\\geq0\\quad(1\\leq j\\leq i<D).$$ The lattice label $m$ is fixed by every tropical step in Equation <a href=\"#tri:incoming-rotation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"tri:incoming-rotation\">[tri:incoming-rotation]</a>. If $m$ is pure, meaning that all its bridge coordinates vanish, then $m=\\sum_i\\mu_i d_i$, where $\\mu_1\\geq\\cdots\\geq\\mu_D\\geq0$ and $\\sum_i\\mu_i=N$.",
        "source_id": "PM-169.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-tri-stationary",
          "exact_quote": "Let $P$ be a homogeneous symmetric polynomial of degree $N$, and suppose $A_m(P(Y))\\ne0$. If $m$ is pure, meaning that all its bridge coordinates vanish, then $m=\\sum_i\\mu_i d_i$, where $\\mu_1\\geq\\cdots\\geq\\mu_D\\geq0$ and $\\sum_i\\mu_i=N$.",
          "source_id": "PM-169.1"
        }

        "exact_quote": "For every partition $\\mu\\vdash N$ with at most $D$ parts, padded by zeroes, put $x_\\mu=\\sum_i\\mu_i d_i$. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **natural unit interval graph** ([source](#main:csf)): The associated *natural unit interval graph* has vertex set $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and edges $$E(G)=\bigl\{\{i,j\}:i<j\le h(i)\bigr\}.$$
- **proper coloring** ([source](#main:csf)): A proper coloring is a map $f:[n]\to\mathbb N_{>0}$ that has distinct values on adjacent vertices.
- **elementary symmetric function and elementary product** ([source](#main:csf)): Here $e_k(X)=\sum_{i_1<\cdots<i_k}x_{i_1}\cdots x_{i_k}$ and $e_\lambda=\prod_i e_{\lambda_i}$ for a partition $\lambda$.
- **lattice of an ordered graph** ([source](#pm-h-7)): Let $L_\Gamma$ be the free abelian group on its vertices, identifying a vertex with its basis vector.
- **elementary units** ([source](#alg:torus)): We call these *elementary units*.
- **admissible list of units** ([source](#alg:torus)): A list of their occurrences is *admissible* if, for each charge bound $B$, their parameters $k$ are bounded above and only finitely many occurrences with $c(p)\le B$ have $k\ge L$, for each $L\in\mathbb Z$.
- **chart** ([source](#pm-h-18)): A *chart* is a finite set $C\subset M$ contained in a lattice basis.
- **wall** ([source](#main:identification)): A wall is a portion of a hyperplane in the real dual of the exponent lattice, equipped with conjugation by a formal series.
- **anchors** ([source](#main:identification)): The auxiliary graph is arranged in a triangle with distinguished independent vertices $d_1,\ldots,d_D$, called anchors.
- **level and row** ([source](#tri:phase-order)): The index $i$ is the *level*, and $j$ is the *row*.
- **decorated packet** ([source](#route:product-energy)): A decorated $k$-packet in $H$ is a triple $(a,e,t)$ with $1\leq t\leq c_k^H(a,e)$.
- **required and exact mask** ([source](#route:masks)): For a subset $J\subseteq[N-1]$, a *required* mask means $J\subseteq\mathcal P_G(w)$ or $J\subseteq\mathcal Q(s)$; an *exact* mask means equality.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-history-energy-upper | energy of a history over a partition | ≤ | $M$ | all partitions of N with at most D parts |
| q-history-energy-lower | energy of a history over a partition | ≥ | $-M$ | all partitions of N with at most D parts |
| q-coefficient-degree-bound | q-degree of an elementary coefficient | ≤ | $M$ | every partition of n |
| q-word-energy | energy of a word as a product state | = | $2\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}_G(w)-M$ | words on the selected vertices |
| q-reversal-inversions | edge inversions of a reversed permutation | = | $M-\mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G(\sigma)$ | permutations in the nondescent domain |
| q-shape-reciprocity | elementary coefficient c_lambda(q) | = | $q^M c_\lambda(q^{-1})$ | all partitions |
| q-history-length-bound | number of nonzero jumps in a history | ≤ | $h(R)$ | every history from the all-negative chamber |
| q-fiber-multiplicity-bound | multiplicity k of the mutating root in a non-pure root | ≤ | $M_\sigma(u)$ | both signs of the side |
| q-output-parameter | parameter of the elementary unit from a primitive string | = | $u(d)-2h$ | nonzero dimension vectors d |
| q-parameter-upper-bound | upper bound on an output unit parameter | ≤ | $BK+B+AB^2$ | charge at most B, input parameters bounded by K, pairings bounded by A |
| q-energy-upper-window | energy of a term in a product of units | ≤ | $BK'+A'B^2$ | parameters bounded by K' and pairings by A' |
| q-unit-coefficient | coefficient of the j-th power term of the simple unit | = | $\frac{v^{-j^2}}{\prod_{i=1}^j(1-v^{-2i})}$ | all nonnegative j |
| q-incoming-mutation-map | incoming label under mutation | = | $n+[-\Omega(p,n)]_+p$ | all lattice labels n |
| q-pack-hilbert-series | Hilbert series of the pack-symmetric polynomial space | = | $\prod_i\prod_{j=1}^{d_i}(1-t^j)^{-1}$ | dimension vectors d |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-insertion-digits** [claim](#stmt-graph-digits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-graph-digits): Delete the largest letter of an admissible word and reinsert it at an allowed position; count new inversions by earlier neighbors to its right. -> bijection with bounded digit sequences and the reversal relation
- **step-commutation** [claim](#stmt-graph-commutation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-graph-commutation): Exchange memberships on odd path components of a pair of independent sets, keeping exponent and skew weight. -> commuting independent-set sums
- **step-kernel** [claim](#stmt-graph-kernel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-graph-kernel): Expand a product over colors, identify proper colorings with disjoint independent sets, then apply the dual Cauchy kernel. -> elementary coefficients as evaluated monomial coefficients
- **step-reciprocity** [claim](#stmt-graph-reciprocity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-graph-reciprocity): Reverse colors, use symmetry from the kernel, and compare elementary coefficients. -> equality with the chromatic function and shape reciprocity
- **step-ordered-decomposition** [claim](#stmt-alg-ordered) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-ordered): Induct on dimension using polygons of destabilizing cuts for spanning, and minimal polygons with restrictions for independence. -> ordered decomposition of shuffle spaces
- **step-slope-restrictions** [claim](#stmt-alg-restrictions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-restrictions): Show destabilizing products are killed by a Taylor test and reduce same-slope products to quotients. -> restriction maps on a slope
- **step-symbol-detection** [claim](#stmt-alg-detection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-detection): Cancel denominators by coarse filtration bounds, then use joint injectivity and tensor detection. -> polynomial symbols detecting graded pieces
- **step-product-symbol** [claim](#stmt-alg-product-symbol) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-product-symbol): Expand intergroup kernels at separated centers and keep the leading grade. -> leading symbol of products
- **step-graded-coproduct** [claim](#stmt-alg-coproduct) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-coproduct): Expand at infinite separation, show negative powers vanish by detection, evaluate at zero. -> graded coproduct components
- **step-bialgebra** [claim](#stmt-alg-bialgebra) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-bialgebra): Test coassociativity, signed cocommutativity and multiplicativity against symbols. -> connected graded bialgebra
- **step-signed-pbw** [claim](#stmt-alg-pbw) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-pbw): Use the convolution logarithm to land in primitives, exponentiate to span, and use iterated coproducts for independence. -> signed PBW isomorphism
- **step-primitive-strings** [claim](#stmt-alg-strings) / [proof](#proof-stmt-alg-strings): Use differentiation and mean multiplication with a commutator relation to split primitives into free strings. -> string decomposition
- **step-positive-reordering** [claim](#stmt-alg-reordering) / [proof](#pm-h-16): Read elementary units off primitive strings, then bound parameters and energies to pass to admissible infinite lists. -> positive reordering of units
- **step-wall-unit** [claim](#stmt-wall-unit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-wall-unit): Count index lists for the coefficient formula and use the commutation relation for conjugation. -> unit normalization and positive crossing
- **step-wall-factors** [claim](#stmt-wall-factors) / [proof](#proof-stmt-wall-factors): Induct on root degree along generic lines, applying the reordering theorem at planar joints. -> positive factorization of wall elements
- **step-coefficient-recursion** [claim](#stmt-wall-recursion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-wall-recursion): Sum crossing differences along a line from the incoming direction to the target chamber. -> positive coefficient recursion
- **step-positive-sections** [claim](#stmt-wall-positivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-wall-positivity): Induct on addition order in a finite interval using the recursion. -> positivity of theta sections
- **step-positive-products** [claim](#stmt-wall-product) / [proof](#proof-stmt-wall-product): Evaluate the product near the incoming direction and use positivity of each factor. -> positive structure coefficients
- **step-fiber-bound** [claim](#stmt-wall-fiber-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-wall-fiber-bound): Induct on non-p degree, tracking upstream and downstream sides of the cut with conjugation by the simple unit. -> finite support in each fiber
- **step-change-of-chart** [claim](#stmt-wall-mutation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-wall-mutation): Compare both completions in a common rational ring and match actual finite coefficients near the cut. -> mutation of wall systems and incoming labels
- **step-embedding** [claim](#stmt-tri-embedding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tri-embedding): Choose rational times step by step in nonempty open intervals, then assign phases by fractional part. -> embedding in a triangular chart
- **step-rotation-comparison** [claim](#stmt-tri-rotation-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tri-rotation-comparison): Verify conjugation on elementary sums by cases on the three moving vertices. -> polynomial comparison under rotation
- **step-stationary-labels** [claim](#stmt-tri-stationary) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tri-stationary): Track a nonzero label through rotations; monotone coordinates, a deficit bound and concavity force vanishing level sums. -> stationary label conditions
- **step-pure-extraction** [claim](#stmt-tri-pure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tri-pure): After many periods choose a test covector so that no wall can change the target coefficient, then read the anchor monomials. -> pure incoming coefficients
- **step-linear-section** [claim](#stmt-tri-linear) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tri-linear): Solve the gap recurrence to show only the first anchor label survives. -> linear section identity
- **step-vertex-domination** [claim](#stmt-tri-domination) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tri-domination): Expand a power of the linear section in theta sections and keep one positive summand. -> domination bound
- **step-monomial-sections** [claim](#stmt-tri-monomial) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tri-monomial): Subtract the theta section and use a minimal-height remaining label to derive a contradiction. -> monomial functions equal theta sections
- **step-elementary-positivity** [claim](#stmt-tri-positivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tri-positivity): Combine the embedding, coloring kernel, reciprocity and monomial identity with positivity of theta sections. -> elementary positivity
- **step-effective-slices** [claim](#stmt-route-finite) / [proof](#proof-stmt-route-finite): Bound packet offsets and jumps by the cone interval and bound energies from both sides. -> finite effective slices
- **step-packet-switch** [claim](#stmt-route-switch) / [proof](#proof-stmt-route-switch): Open positive and negative packets on opposite sides and match jump tuples by rank using the automorphism property. -> packet switch bijection
- **step-return-to-anchors** [claim](#stmt-route-return) / [proof](#proof-stmt-route-return): Compare coefficients at a pure label and force all gains along a test path to vanish, then apply zero-jump switches. -> transport to anchor tuples
- **step-degree-bounds** [claim](#stmt-route-parity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-route-parity): Identify the history enumerator with the theta coefficient and use the elementary positivity corollary. -> energy range and parity
- **step-exact-mask** [claim](#stmt-route-exact) / [proof](#proof-stmt-route-exact): Recursively define exact-mask bijections by following alternating paths in two matchings. -> exact-mask bijections
- **step-permutation-witness** [claim](#stmt-route-witness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-route-witness): Specialize to the empty mask so shapes correspond to weakly decreasing anchor words, and sum energies. -> permutation witness identity
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-main-theorem) / [proof](#proof-stmt-route-witness-2): Reverse the input permutation, apply the witness map, and use reciprocity to convert admissible-word inversions to graph inversions. -> elementary positivity with permutation witness
- **step-hessenberg-modules** [claim](#stmt-main-hessenberg) / [proof](#proof-stmt-main-hessenberg): Apply the cohomology character identity, the main expansion, and injectivity of the Frobenius characteristic. -> module isomorphism

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-digit-product-distribution**: For a natural unit interval graph, the generating polynomial of the graph-inversion statistic over the permutations with no nonedge adjacent descent equals the product over vertices a of the geometric sums 1+q+...+q^{l_a}, where l_a counts the earlier neighbours of a. This follows from the insertion-digit bijection together with reversal, which complements graph inversions against the edge count and leaves the symmetric product unchanged.
- **inv-digit-sum**: Under the insertion-digit bijection, the edge-inversion count of an admissible word equals the sum of its digits.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-polynomial-time-claim**: Treating the permutation-to-partition witness procedure as a polynomial-time routine and deploying it inside a time-bounded service. -> The source proves only termination of the finite procedure; no running-time bound is given, so latency guarantees do not follow.
- **anti-geometric-basis**: Reading the module decomposition of Hessenberg cohomology as a canonical geometric basis or as compatible with cup product. -> The isomorphism is abstract: it is obtained from equality of characters and complete reducibility, so it carries no preferred basis and no ring structure.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The insertion-digit lemma and its reversal identity give a finite computation: the graph-inversion generating polynomial over permutations without nonedge adjacent descents is produced from the earlier-neighbour counts and checked against brute-force enumeration on small graphs. This is a finite constructive consequence of the paper's lemma, not the full witness map.

```python
# INPUTS: stdin: first token n (number of vertices), then h(1) ... h(n), a weakly increasing sequence with i <= h(i) <= n.
# PRECONDITIONS: h is weakly increasing with i <= h(i) <= n; n >= 0.
# OUTPUTS: stdout: the coefficients of the graph-inversion generating polynomial over D_G^0, from degree 0 up to degree M, space separated.
# TIME: O(n^2 + n * M * n) arithmetic steps for the digit convolution.
# SPACE: O(M) integers.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers, no rounding.
# FAILURE: malformed input is not handled; the program raises an exception and exits non-zero.
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    n = int(data[0])
    h = [int(x) for x in data[1:1 + n]]
    poly = [1]
    for a in range(1, n + 1):
        l = sum(1 for b in range(1, a) if h[b - 1] >= a)
        new = [0] * (len(poly) + l)
        for i, c in enumerate(poly):
            for j in range(l + 1):
                new[i + j] += c
        poly = new
    print(" ".join(map(str, poly)))

main()
```

    "oracle_code": "import sys, itertools\n\ndef main():\n    inp = open(sys.argv[1]).read().split()\n    n = int(inp[0])\n    h = [int(x) for x in inp[1:1 + n]]\n    def adj(a, b):\n        a, b = min(a, b), max(a, b)\n        return b <= h[a - 1]\n    M = sum(1 for a in range(1, n + 1) for b in range(a + 1, n + 1) if adj(a, b))\n    cnt = [0] * (M + 1)\n    for s in itertools.permutations(range(1, n + 1)):\n        ok = all((not (s[i] > s[i + 1])) or adj(s[i], s[i + 1]) for i in range(n - 1))\n        if ok:\n            g = sum(1 for i in range(n) for j in range(i + 1, n) if s[i] > s[j] and adj(s[i], s[j]))\n            cnt[g] += 1\n    got = open(sys.argv[2]).read().split()\n    exp = sys.argv[3].split()\n    want = [str(c) for c in cnt]\n    sys.exit(0 if (got == want and exp == want) else 1)\n\nmain()\n",
    "output_contract": "One line with M+1 non-negative integers, the coefficient of q^d being the number of permutations in D_G^0 with graph-inversion count d, where M is the edge count.",
    "reason": "The insertion-digit lemma and its reversal identity give a finite computation: the graph-inversion generating polynomial over permutations without nonedge adjacent descents is produced from the earlier-neighbour counts and checked against brute-force enumeration on small graphs. This is a finite constructive consequence of the paper's lemma, not the full witness map.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: stdin: first token n (number of vertices), then h(1) ... h(n), a weakly increasing sequence with i <= h(i) <= n.\n# PRECONDITIONS: h is weakly increasing with i <= h(i) <= n; n >= 0.\n# OUTPUTS: stdout: the coefficients of the graph-inversion generating polynomial over D_G^0, from degree 0 up to degree M, space separated.\n# TIME: O(n^2 + n * M * n) arithmetic steps for the digit convolution.\n# SPACE: O(M) integers.\n# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers, no rounding.\n# FAILURE: malformed input is not handled; the program raises an exception and exits non-zero.\nimport sys\n\ndef main():\n    data = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    n = int(data[0])\n    h = [int(x) for x in data[1:1 + n]]\n    poly = [1]\n    for a in range(1, n + 1):\n        l = sum(1 for b in range(1, a) if h[b - 1] >= a)\n        new = [0] * (len(poly) + l)\n        for i, c in enumerate(poly):\n            for j in range(l + 1):\n                new[i + j] += c\n        poly = new\n    print(\" \".join(map(str, poly)))\n\nmain()\n",
    "test_vectors": [
      {
        "expected": "1",
        "input": "0\n"
      },
      {
        "expected": "1",
        "input": "1\n1\n"
      },
      {
        "expected": "1",
        "input": "2\n1 2\n"
      },
      {
        "expected": "1 2 1",
        "input": "3\n2 3 3\n"
      },
      {
        "expected": "1 1",
        "input": "2\n2 2\n"
      },
      {
        "expected": "1 2 2 1",
        "input": "3\n3 3 3\n"
      },
      {
        "expected": "1 3 3 1",
        "input": "4\n2 3 4 4\n"
      }
    ]
  },

}

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper gives a mathematical procedure with termination but no complexity bound, so there is no ready software module; the procedure is only a candidate for an exact certificate generator. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No simulation, game or physical dynamics is modelled; the objects are graphs, permutations and symmetric functions. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A symbolic checker of elementary expansions of chromatic functions is conceivable, but no tool construction is stated, so it stays inferred and not applicable. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A deterministic terminating procedure exists, but its steps rely on wall recursions and alternating matchings with no stated complexity; this is too heavy to give honest pseudocode. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is proposed. Only finite sets of histories and masks appear, as proof devices. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No parallel or hardware mapping is discussed and the procedure is a sequential recursion. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, protocol or security notion appears in the paper. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning, data or statistical model appears; the results are exact algebraic identities. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective function or optimisation problem is posed; the paper is about positivity of coefficients. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No concurrency, communication or consensus structure appears. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a pure combinatorial-algebraic theorem about chromatic quasisymmetric functions, so it has no direct engineering use beyond symbolic verification. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-no-polynomial-time** (formal_scope): The witness map is proved to terminate on every input, but the source gives no polynomial-time bound, so no running-time guarantee carries over. Blocked use: ['Deploying the witness procedure inside a latency-bounded or real-time service', 'Quoting a polynomial complexity bound for the permutation-to-partition map']
- **limit-graph-class** (hypothesis): All statements are made for natural unit interval graphs, defined by a weakly increasing bound function h with i at most h(i) at most n. Nothing is asserted for other graph classes. Blocked use: ['Applying the positivity result or the witness map to a graph outside this class', 'Using a different vertex labelling than the natural one']
- **limit-abstract-isomorphism** (formal_scope): The Hessenberg cohomology decomposition is an abstract module isomorphism obtained from equality of characters and complete reducibility. It carries no preferred basis and no ring compatibility. Blocked use: ['Reading the decomposition as a canonical geometric basis', 'Computing cup products or intersection numbers from the decomposition']
- **limit-effective-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The effective part of the positive reordering theorem requires effectively computable lattice operations, form and charge, together with a computable pairing bound for every charge cutoff. Without these, only existence of the factorization is asserted. Blocked use: ['Running the coefficient computation for a monoid whose form or charge is not computable', 'Assuming a pairing bound that has not been supplied']
- **limit-required-versus-exact-mask** (edge_case): A required mask only asks containment in the ascent set of a word, while an exact mask asks equality. The required-mask bijection cannot be used in place of the exact-mask one; the cancellation step is what converts between them. Blocked use: ['Taking the required-mask bijection as the final partition assignment', 'Skipping the alternating-path cancellation stage']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 169 (`lean/docs/169.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Elementary positivity of chromatic quasisymmetric functions](../../preprints/Elementary-Positivity-of-Chromatic-Quasisymmetric-Functions-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The elementary-positivity part of the Shareshian–Wachs conjecture asks whether the chromatic quasisymmetric function of every natural unit interval graph has nonnegative coefficients in the elementary basis. The formalization proves this over $\mathbb N[q]$. It constructs an explicit elementary-basis expansion indexed by the graph's permitted nondescent permutations, with each term weighted by a nonnegative power of $q$ determined by its graph inversions. The expansion holds for every finite number of color variables.

Comparator source `ElementaryPositivity.lean` (2174 bytes, sha256 `7cdb9739c3f71939cb78efb4472721a95eda2c15cdf99e7ab2c8038b69f07bef`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace ElementaryPositivity

structure NaturalUnitIntervalGraph (n : ℕ) where
  h : Fin n → Fin n
  increasing : Monotone h
  extensive : ∀ i, i ≤ h i

namespace NaturalUnitIntervalGraph

variable {n : ℕ} (G : NaturalUnitIntervalGraph n)

def Edge (i j : Fin n) : Prop :=
  (i < j ∧ j ≤ G.h i) ∨ (j < i ∧ i ≤ G.h j)

instance (i j : Fin n) : Decidable (G.Edge i j) :=
  inferInstanceAs (Decidable ((_ ∧ _) ∨ (_ ∧ _)))

def Nondescent (σ : Equiv.Perm (Fin n)) : Prop :=
  ∀ i j : Fin n, i.val + 1 = j.val → σ j < σ i → G.Edge (σ i) (σ j)

instance (σ : Equiv.Perm (Fin n)) : Decidable (G.Nondescent σ) :=
  inferInstanceAs (Decidable (∀ i j : Fin n,
    i.val + 1 = j.val → σ j < σ i → G.Edge (σ i) (σ j)))

def graphInversions (σ : Equiv.Perm (Fin n)) : ℕ :=
  (Finset.univ.filter fun ij : Fin n × Fin n =>
    ij.1 < ij.2 ∧ σ ij.2 < σ ij.1 ∧ G.Edge (σ ij.1) (σ ij.2)).card

def Proper {r : ℕ} (f : Fin n → Fin r) : Prop :=
  ∀ i j : Fin n, G.Edge i j → f i ≠ f j

instance {r : ℕ} (f : Fin n → Fin r) : Decidable (G.Proper f) :=
  inferInstanceAs (Decidable (∀ i j : Fin n, G.Edge i j → f i ≠ f j))

def coloringAscents {r : ℕ} (f : Fin n → Fin r) : ℕ :=
  (Finset.univ.filter fun ij : Fin n × Fin n =>
    ij.1 < ij.2 ∧ G.Edge ij.1 ij.2 ∧ f ij.1 < f ij.2).card

noncomputable def chromatic (r : ℕ) : MvPolynomial (Fin r) (Polynomial ℕ) :=
  ∑ f ∈ Finset.univ.filter (G.Proper (r := r)),
    MvPolynomial.C (Polynomial.X ^ G.coloringAscents f) *
      ∏ i : Fin n, MvPolynomial.X (f i)

structure PermutationWitness where
  theta : {σ : Equiv.Perm (Fin n) // G.Nondescent σ} → Nat.Partition n
  expansion : ∀ r : ℕ, G.chromatic r =
    ∑ σ : {σ : Equiv.Perm (Fin n) // G.Nondescent σ},
      MvPolynomial.C (Polynomial.X ^ G.graphInversions σ.val) *
        MvPolynomial.esymmPart (Fin r) (Polynomial ℕ) (theta σ)

end NaturalUnitIntervalGraph
end ElementaryPositivity

def elementaryPositivityWitness (n : ℕ)
    (G : ElementaryPositivity.NaturalUnitIntervalGraph n) :
    G.PermutationWitness := by
  sorry

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the chromatic quasisymmetric function of every natural unit interval graph is elementary-positive over $`\mathbb N[q]`$. This resolves the elementary-positivity part of the Shareshian–Wachs conjecture.

<a id="main:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="main:csf"></a>
Let $h=(h(1),\ldots,h(n))$ be a weakly increasing integer sequence with $i\le h(i)\le n$. The associated *natural unit interval graph* has vertex set $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and edges $$E(G)=\bigl\{\{i,j\}:i<j\le h(i)\bigr\}.$$ These are also called naturally labeled Dyck graphs. A proper coloring is a map $f:[n]\to\mathbb N_{>0}$ that has distinct values on adjacent vertices. In the variable alphabet $X=(x_1,x_2,\ldots)$ put $$\chi_G(X;q)=\sum_{f\text{ proper}}
 q^{\operatorname{asc}_G(f)}\prod_{a=1}^n x_{f(a)},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{asc}_G(f)=
 \#\{\{a,b\}\in E(G):a<b,\ f(a)<f(b)\}.
 \label{main:csf}$$ Shareshian and Wachs proved that this function is symmetric for this graph class and conjectured that its coefficients in the elementary basis belong to $\mathbb N[q]$ (Shareshian and Wachs 2016) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[SW16](#ref-SW16)]. Here $e_k(X)=\sum_{i_1<\cdots<i_k}x_{i_1}\cdots x_{i_k}$ and $e_\lambda=\prod_i e_{\lambda_i}$ for a partition $\lambda$.

The coefficients admit a more precise interpretation in terms of permutations. Define $$\begin{aligned}
 D_G^0&=\{\sigma\in S_n:
   \sigma_i>\sigma_{i+1}\Longrightarrow
   \{\sigma_i,\sigma_{i+1}\}\in E(G)\},\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G(\sigma)&=\#\{(i,j):i<j,\ \sigma_i>\sigma_j,
                    \{\sigma_i,\sigma_j\}\in E(G)\}.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $D_G^0$ forbids adjacent descents between nonneighbors, and $\mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G$ counts only inversions supported on edges.

<a id="stmt-main-theorem"></a>
<div id="main:theorem" class="theorem">

<a id="main:witness"></a>
**Theorem 1** (Elementary positivity and a permutation witness). *There is a deterministic terminating procedure which, given a natural unit interval graph $G$ on $[n]$ and $\sigma\in D_G^0$, returns a partition $\theta_G(\sigma)\vdash n$ such that $$\chi_G(X;q)=\sum_{\sigma\in D_G^0}
       q^{\mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G(\sigma)}e_{\theta_G(\sigma)}(X).
 \label{main:witness}$$ In particular every elementary coefficient of $\chi_G(X;q)$ belongs to $\mathbb N[q]$. The assertion holds as a formal symmetric-function identity, uniformly in $n$ and $G$.*

</div>

This proves the elementary-positivity part of the Shareshian–Wachs conjecture. The theorem makes no assertion of elementary unimodality. The construction specifies a map on individual inputs: it does not take the elementary coefficient list as input and redistribute permutations according to that list. Its running time may nevertheless be large.

<a id="main:path-example"></a>
For example, let $G$ be the path $1$–$2$–$3$. Its expansion is $$\chi_G(X;q)=q\,e_{(2,1)}(X)+(1+q+q^2)e_{(3)}(X).
 \label{main:path-example}$$ Write $m_\lambda$ for the sum of the distinct monomials with exponent multiset $\lambda$. To check this directly, the colorings with a repeated color contribute $q\,m_{(2,1)}(X)$, while those with three distinct colors contribute $(1+4q+q^2)m_{(1,1,1)}(X)$. Now use $e_{(2,1)}=m_{(2,1)}+3m_{(1,1,1)}$ and $e_{(3)}=m_{(1,1,1)}$. The domain $D_G^0$ consists of $123,132,213,321$, with graph-inversion degrees $0,1,1,2$. The theorem assigns one of the two degree-one permutations to $(2,1)$ and the other three permutations to $(3)$. Thus the map refines the degree count by an entire elementary partition. Equation <a href="#main:path-example" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:path-example">[main:path-example]</a> alone does not specify which of $132$ and $213$ receives which partition; the procedure in Section <a href="#route:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="route:section">6</a> makes that individual choice.

The positivity question is difficult because the coloring definition already has nonnegative coefficients in the monomial variables, whereas rewriting it in the elementary basis can involve subtraction. Our proof separates this issue from the individual assignment. It first identifies each relevant elementary coefficient with a positive algebraic object. It then realizes the algebraic identities by finite bijections between ordered collections of independent sets. Neither stage uses the sought elementary coefficient list as input to the permutation rule.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and significance

The problem grew out of positivity questions for immanants of Jacobi–Trudi matrices, studied by Haiman and by Stanley and Stembridge (Haiman 1993; Stanley and Stembridge 1993) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Hai93](#ref-Hai93), [SS93](#ref-SS93)]. Stanley introduced the chromatic symmetric function as a symmetric-function refinement of the chromatic polynomial (Stanley 1995) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Sta95](#ref-Sta95)]; it is the specialization of Equation <a href="#main:csf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:csf">[main:csf]</a> at $q=1$. In this language, the Stanley–Stembridge conjecture asks for elementary positivity of the incomparability graphs of $(3+1)$-free posets (Stanley and Stembridge 1993, Conjecture 5.5) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[SS93](#ref-SS93)]. The incomparability graph joins two elements exactly when neither is below the other. A poset is $(3+1)$-free if it has no induced three-element chain together with an element incomparable to that chain. Guay-Paquet’s reduction expresses its chromatic symmetric function as a nonnegative rational combination of those of unit interval orders (Guay-Paquet 2013, Theorem 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[GP13](#ref-GP13)]. Their naturally labeled incomparability graphs are the graphs in Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a>. Specializing our theorem at $q=1$ therefore also proves the classical positivity theorem, previously established by Hikita (Hikita 2025) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Hik24](#ref-Hik24)].

Positive expansions in other bases provided important earlier structure. Haiman’s immanant results imply Schur positivity for incomparability graphs of $(3+1)$-free posets (Haiman 1993) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Hai93](#ref-Hai93)]; Gasharov gave a positive formula using poset tableaux (Gasharov 1996) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Gas96](#ref-Gas96)]. Chow’s quasisymmetric expansion related chromatic symmetric functions to permutation descents (Chow 1999) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Cho99](#ref-Cho99)]. Shareshian and Wachs introduced the $q$-refinement in Equation <a href="#main:csf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:csf">[main:csf]</a>, proved its symmetry and Schur positivity for natural unit interval graphs, and formulated the elementary-positivity and elementary-unimodality conjecture (Shareshian and Wachs 2012, 2016) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[SW12](#ref-SW12), [SW16](#ref-SW16)]. Their acyclic-orientation formula gives elementary coefficients after grouping partitions by their number of parts (Shareshian and Wachs 2016, Theorem 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[SW16](#ref-SW16)]; Athanasiadis supplied a power-sum expansion (Athanasiadis 2015) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Ath15](#ref-Ath15)]. These descriptions do not determine a nonnegative coefficient formula for each elementary partition.

The geometric connection gives another reason to study the $q$-grading. Tymoczko constructed permutation actions on equivariant cohomology (Tymoczko 2008) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Tym08](#ref-Tym08)], and Shareshian and Wachs related their chromatic functions conjecturally to the graded representation on regular semisimple Hessenberg varieties (Shareshian and Wachs 2012, 2016) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[SW12](#ref-SW12), [SW16](#ref-SW16)]. Brosnan–Chow and Guay-Paquet proved this representation-theoretic identity (Brosnan and Chow 2018; Guay-Paquet 2016) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BC18](#ref-BC18), [GP16](#ref-GP16)]. That result is distinct from the elementary-positivity assertion addressed here. Geometric, combinatorial and algebraic approaches subsequently established positivity for substantial families: abelian Hessenberg varieties (Harada and Precup 2019) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[HP19](#ref-HP19)], classes treated by explicit elementary expansions (Cho and Huh 2019) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[CH19](#ref-CH19)], melting lollipops (Huh, Nam, and Yoo 2020) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[HNY20](#ref-HNY20)], and the $q$-rook formulas for abelian Dyck paths (Colmenarejo, Morales, and Panova 2023) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[CMP23](#ref-CMP23)]. The modular law also provided a systematic tool for organizing chromatic functions and their relations (Abreu and Nigro 2021) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[AN21](#ref-AN21)].

Hikita’s proof uses a probabilistic formula (Hikita 2025) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Hik24](#ref-Hik24)]. His formula gives numerical nonnegativity for every positive real $q$, while its rational $q$-weights do not establish coefficientwise nonnegativity in $\mathbb N[q]$; the refined conjecture remains explicitly distinguished in (Hikita 2025, sec. 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Hik24](#ref-Hik24)]. Griffin, Mellit, Romero, Weigl and Wen gave an independent proof through Macdonald expansions, recovering Hikita’s elementary formula (Griffin et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[GMRWW25](#ref-GMRWW25)]. Further developments include positivity at $q=1$ of the Abreu–Nigro $g$-functions, proved by Huh, Hwang, Kim, Kim and Oh as a refinement of Hikita’s theorem (Huh et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[HHKKO25](#ref-HHKKO25)], Chow’s combinatorial interpretation of the probabilities (Chow 2026) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Chow26](#ref-Chow26)], and lower bounds for elementary coefficients studied by Siegl (Siegl 2026) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Siegl26](#ref-Siegl26)]. Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a> gives a formal polynomial formula with nonnegative integer coefficients and an individual assignment preserving the specified graph-inversion weight.

The representation identity and Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a> give the following degreewise consequence.

<a id="stmt-main-hessenberg"></a>
<div id="main:hessenberg" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Young permutation modules in Hessenberg cohomology). *Let $n\ge1$, let $h$ be as above with graph $G=G(h)$, and let $S\in M_n(\mathbb C)$ be regular semisimple. Write $$\operatorname{Hess}(S,h)=
 \{F_\bullet\in\operatorname{Flag}(\mathbb C^n):
     SF_i\subseteq F_{h(i)}\text{ for all }i\}.$$ For $\lambda=(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_\ell)\vdash n$, let $M^\lambda=\operatorname{Ind}_{S_\lambda}^{S_n}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb C}$ be the Young permutation module, where $S_\lambda=S_{\lambda_1}\times\cdots\times S_{\lambda_\ell}$ is the standard Young subgroup, and put $$m_{\lambda,d}(h)=
 \#\{\sigma\in D_G^0:\theta_G(\sigma)=\lambda,\ \mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G(\sigma)=d\}.$$ Then for every integer $d\ge0$, under Tymoczko’s dot action on ordinary cohomology there is an abstract $\mathbb C[S_n]$-module isomorphism $$H^{2d}(\operatorname{Hess}(S,h);\mathbb C)
 \cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash n}(M^\lambda)^{\oplus m_{\lambda,d}(h)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-main-hessenberg"><summary>Proof of stmt-main-hessenberg</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\operatorname{ch}$ for the Frobenius characteristic and $\omega$ for the usual symmetric-function involution, so $\omega(e_\lambda)=h_\lambda$, the product of complete homogeneous symmetric functions. The identity of Brosnan–Chow (Brosnan and Chow 2018, Theorem 129) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[BC18](#ref-BC18)] and Guay-Paquet (Guay-Paquet 2016) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[GP16](#ref-GP16)], followed by Equation <a href="#main:witness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:witness">[main:witness]</a>, gives $$\operatorname{ch}H^{2d}(\operatorname{Hess}(S,h);\mathbb C)
 =[q^d]\,\omega\chi_G(X;q)
 =\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}m_{\lambda,d}(h)h_\lambda.$$ Since $\operatorname{ch}M^\lambda=h_\lambda$ (Brosnan and Chow 2018, proof of Proposition 10) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[BC18](#ref-BC18)], injectivity of the Frobenius characteristic and complete reducibility of finite-dimensional complex $S_n$-modules give the isomorphism. The argument supplies no preferred isomorphism, geometric basis, or compatibility with cup product. ◻

</details>

There is also a direct precedent for asking for a partition-valued permutation rule. Stanley and Stembridge proposed such an assignment on their compatible permutations (Stanley and Stembridge 1993, Remark 5.6) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[SS93](#ref-SS93)]. That proposal concerns the ungraded elementary expansion. The rule here retains the full graph-inversion degree and specifies a terminating procedure on each input. The finite cancellation used to pass from required ascents to exact ascents belongs to the bijective-cancellation tradition represented by the Garsia–Milne involution principle (Garsia and Milne 1981) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[GM81](#ref-GM81)].

Our algebraic reduction likewise has substantial antecedents. Stanley’s graph specialization (Stanley 1998, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Sta98](#ref-Sta98)], chromatic functions in noncommuting variables (Gebhard and Sagan 2001) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[GS01](#ref-GS01)], and the noncommutative symmetric-function methods of Fomin–Greene and Blasiak–Fomin (Fomin and Greene 1998; Blasiak and Fomin 2017) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[FG98](#ref-FG98), [BF17](#ref-BF17)] provide a framework for commuting symmetric expressions inside a noncommutative algebra. Hwang’s noncommutative $P$-symmetric functions (Hwang 2024) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Hwa24](#ref-Hwa24)] and the poset algebras of Blasiak, Eriksson, Pylyavskyy and Siegl (Blasiak et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[BEPS25](#ref-BEPS25)] provide direct predecessors of the graph-alphabet evaluation and its elementary/monomial coefficient pairing. We give a short proof in the centered quantum torus conventions needed for the subsequent argument.

The positive factorization and transport stages use the mathematical framework of Hall algebras and scattering diagrams. Ordered slope factorization and polynomial shuffle products were developed in (Reineke 2003; Kontsevich and Soibelman 2011) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Rei03](#ref-Rei03), [KS11](#ref-KS11)], with semistable quotient and integrality results in (Efimov 2012; Franzen and Reineke 2018; Davison and Meinhardt 2020) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Ef12](#ref-Ef12), [FR18](#ref-FR18), [DMe20](#ref-DMe20)]. The connection from Hall factorization to wall transformations appears in Reineke’s Poisson automorphisms and Bridgeland’s stability scattering diagrams (Reineke 2010; Bridgeland 2017) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Rei10](#ref-Rei10), [Bri17](#ref-Bri17)]. Scattering constructions originate in work of Kontsevich–Soibelman and Gross–Siebert (Kontsevich and Soibelman 2006, 2014; Gross and Siebert 2011) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[KS06](#ref-KS06), [GS11](#ref-GS11), [KS14](#ref-KS14)]; theta bases and mutation are central to the cluster framework of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich (Gross et al. 2018) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[GHKK18](#ref-GHKK18)], and Davison–Mandel prove strong quantum positivity (Davison and Mandel 2021) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[DM21](#ref-DM21)]. We prove the particular reordering, completion and transport statements needed here with their explicit gradings and finite coefficient bounds. The graph-specific step is then an identification: general positivity of theta sections does not itself show that a graph-alphabet monomial symmetric function is one such section. The triangular chart below proves that identity before the packet construction extracts the individual witness.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof structure

<a id="main:reduction"></a>
The first reduction replaces colorings by coefficients in an algebra whose monomials record vertex multiplicities. Write $q=v^2$ and let $X_m$ denote the basis monomial indexed by a vertex-multiplicity vector $m$. Multiplication of these monomials is twisted by an alternating form recording ordered edges. For a larger unit interval graph $\Gamma$ containing $G$, let $E_k$ be the sum of the monomials of independent $k$-sets. We prove that the $E_k$ commute, so they may be evaluated as elementary symmetric functions $e_k(Y)$ of a formal alphabet $Y$. If $u$ is the sum of the original vertex exponents and $M=|E(G)|$, the elementary coefficient becomes $$\chi_G(X;v^2)
       =v^M[X_u]m_\lambda(Y).
 \label{main:reduction}$$ Here $m_\lambda$ is the monomial symmetric function. The identity is proved in Proposition <a href="#graph:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="graph:kernel">5</a>.

<a id="main:identification"></a>
The central step is to identify $m_\lambda(Y)$ as a chamber value of a single positive section of a system of wall transformations. A wall is a portion of a hyperplane in the real dual of the exponent lattice, equipped with conjugation by a formal series. A section is a family of chamber values related by these transformations. We construct sections $\Theta_m$, indexed by lattice exponents, by prescribing their coefficients in test directions determined by the alternating form. The auxiliary graph is arranged in a triangle with distinguished independent vertices $d_1,\ldots,d_D$, called anchors. For a partition $\lambda$ of length at most $D$, padded by zeroes to length $D$, the essential identification in the all-negative chamber is $$m_\lambda(Y)=\Theta_{\lambda_1d_1+\cdots+\lambda_Dd_D}.
 \label{main:identification}$$ Repeated interval rotations establish this identity. A support argument using positive powers of $E_1$ is essential: stationarity under the rotations alone does not force an exponent to involve anchors only.

The proof has three main components. First, positive quantum factors remain positive when their order is reversed. We realize the new multiplicities as dimensions of explicit graded vector spaces, and prove the coefficient bounds needed for all infinite series. Second, the interval rotations identify a family of graph symmetric functions with single positive theta sections. Together with Equation <a href="#main:reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:reduction">[main:reduction]</a>, this already proves coefficientwise elementary positivity. Third, we extract the individual assignment. After reversing an input permutation, we group vertices into packets, each an independent set written in increasing order. A local packet bijection transports them to subsets of the independent anchors and records weighted jumps. The total jump histories are independent of packet sizes. Finite matching cancellation refines required nonedge ascents of input words and required strict ascents of anchor words to exact sets of such positions. The empty set on the anchor side leaves a weakly decreasing word, whose multiplicities give the output partition. The finite matching argument supplies termination without consulting the desired final shape counts.

Section <a href="#graph:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="graph:section">2</a> proves the coloring reduction and fixes permutation conventions. Section <a href="#alg:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:section">3</a> proves the reordering result. Section <a href="#wall:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wall:section">4</a> constructs the wall sections and proves their positivity and mutation rules. Section <a href="#tri:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tri:section">5</a> establishes Equation <a href="#main:identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:identification">[main:identification]</a>, and Section <a href="#route:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="route:section">6</a> constructs the individual permutation map. Section <a href="#main:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:completion">7</a> returns to the original graph and proves Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a>, including the reversal needed for the convention in $D_G^0$.

<a id="graph:section"></a>
### Graph alphabets and the coloring reduction

This section reduces the elementary expansion to coefficients of monomial symmetric functions evaluated in a quantum torus. It also fixes the permutation convention used by the transport construction.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Admissible words and insertion digits

For the graph $G$ in the introduction put $l_a=\#\{b<a:\{b,a\}\in E(G)\}$ and $M=\sum_a l_a$. The earlier neighbors of $a$ are a suffix of $[a-1]$, and they form a clique. Let $$\mathcal A_G=\{w\in S_n:
    w_i<w_{i+1}\Longrightarrow\{w_i,w_{i+1}\}\in E(G)\}.$$ We call these words *admissible*. Their forbidden adjacent pairs are nonedge ascents. Write $\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}_G(w)$ for their edge-inversion count, given by the same formula as $\mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G$.

<a id="stmt-graph-digits"></a>
<div id="graph:digits" class="lemma">

<a id="graph:reverse"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Insertion digits). *There is a bijection between $\mathcal A_G$ and the sequences $(j_1,\ldots,j_n)$ with $0\le j_a\le l_a$. Under this bijection, $\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}_G(w)=\sum_a j_a$. Reversal is a bijection $D_G^0\to\mathcal A_G$, and $$\mathop{\mathrm{inv}}_G(\operatorname{reverse}\sigma)=M-\mathop{\mathrm{ginv}}_G(\sigma).
 \label{graph:reverse}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-graph-digits"><summary>Proof of stmt-graph-digits</summary>

*Proof.* Delete the largest letter $a$ from an admissible word on $[a]$. If it has predecessor $b$ and successor $c$, then $b<a$ and $h(b)\ge a>c$, so the newly consecutive pair $(b,c)$ is allowed. The cases without a predecessor or successor are immediate. Conversely, $a$ can be inserted at the beginning or immediately after any earlier neighbor, and these are exactly the allowed positions. At such a position the new inversions are the earlier neighbors lying to its right. Reading those neighbors in their order in the shorter word shows that the available counts are $l_a,l_a-1,\ldots,0$, each once. Successive insertion and deletion prove the bijection and degree formula. Reversal exchanges nonedge descents and nonedge ascents and reverses the order of the endpoints of every edge, proving Equation <a href="#graph:reverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="graph:reverse">[graph:reverse]</a>. ◻

</details>

The relation $a<b$ and $\{a,b\}\notin E(G)$ is transitive: it is equivalent to $h(a)<b$. Consequently, an increasing word in which each adjacent pair is a nonedge lists an independent set. This observation will identify packets of independent vertices with required ascent positions in Section <a href="#route:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="route:section">6</a>.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The quantum torus of an ordered graph

For this subsection let $\Gamma$ be any natural unit interval graph on a finite ordered vertex set. Let $L_\Gamma$ be the free abelian group on its vertices, identifying a vertex with its basis vector. Define an integer alternating form by <a id="graph:form"></a>$$\Omega(b,a)=
 \begin{cases}1,&a<b\text{ and }\{a,b\}\in E(\Gamma),\\
                0,&a<b\text{ and }\{a,b\}\notin E(\Gamma),
 \end{cases}
 \label{graph:form}$$ and extend by bilinearity and skew symmetry. Over $\mathbb Q((v^{-1}))$, the quantum torus has basis $X_m$, $m\in L_\Gamma$, and product <a id="graph:torus"></a>$$X_mX_{m'}=v^{\Omega(m,m')}X_{m+m'}.
 \label{graph:torus}$$ The coefficient field consists of Laurent series with bounded upper powers of $v$. All elements considered in this section are finite Laurent polynomials; the additional cone completions will be specified when needed.

For a set $I$ of vertices write $a_I=\sum_{a\in I}a$, and define <a id="graph:elementaries"></a>$$E_k(\Gamma)=\sum_{\substack{I\subseteq V(\Gamma)\text{ independent}\\|I|=k}}
 X_{a_I},\qquad E_0(\Gamma)=1.
 \label{graph:elementaries}$$

The graph alphabet used here is a centered quantum-torus version of noncommutative poset symmetric functions (Hwang 2024; Blasiak et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Hwa24](#ref-Hwa24), [BEPS25](#ref-BEPS25)]. More precisely, on $V(\Gamma)$ define $a<_P b$ when $a<b$ and $\{a,b\}$ is a nonedge. The chain elementary sums in (Blasiak et al. 2025, Equation (2.4)) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[BEPS25](#ref-BEPS25)] become our independent-set sums, and the relations in (Blasiak et al. 2025, Definition 3.16) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[BEPS25](#ref-BEPS25)] become the relations above under $u_a\mapsto X_a$ and $t\mapsto v^2$. We prove commutation directly in these conventions.

<a id="stmt-graph-commutation"></a>
<div id="graph:commutation" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4** (Commuting independent-set sums). *For every natural unit interval graph $\Gamma$ and all $j,k\ge0$, $E_j(\Gamma)E_k(\Gamma)=E_k(\Gamma)E_j(\Gamma)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-graph-commutation"><summary>Proof of stmt-graph-commutation</summary>

*Proof.* We adapt the two-color involution underlying (Shareshian and Wachs 2016, Lemma 4.4 and Theorem 4.5) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[SW16](#ref-SW16)]. A term on the left is an ordered pair of independent sets $(I,J)$, with exponent $a_I+a_J$ and weight $v^{\Omega(a_I,a_J)}$. Vertices in $I\cap J$ have no neighbors in $I\cup J$ and contribute no skew weight. In the remaining induced graph, every earlier-neighbor set and every later-neighbor set is a clique. Since the graph is bipartite with parts $I\setminus J$ and $J\setminus I$, each vertex has at most one earlier and one later neighbor. More explicitly, an edge that skipped a vertex of $I\cup J$ in the total order would make a triangle: the interval-closure property supplies both intervening edges.
